Swans, lovely sarasa cranes — on the bank, by the celestial river;
birds (?) without fear [sit] on the pomegranate trees — flocks of parrots.[^02-45]
Hosts of cuckoos on the mango trees, many cocks — [all] are rapt;
on the fresh nipa trees, hundreds of thousands of peacocks.
All around, hundreds of rows of doves: there is no comparison for them;
flock upon flock, many khanjana wagtails in the lovely kunjas.
Thus, in many ways — who can describe [it]? There is no end [to it].
[All] display their gifts; Narahari proclaims [only] a small part.

*Mala-chhandah*

**2.20.** The residents of the town of Sri Navadvipa — people who are [embodied] heaps of happiness,
bees, beasts and birds, grass, trees, shrubs and the rest —
[their] hearts [are turned] to the beautiful deeds of Gaurachandra; [their] course is the highest prema.
[They] are wholly aprakrita (not of this world), though they look just like the prakrita (the mundane).[^02-46]

*Raga bhairava*

**2.14a.** Today, at the close of night, [this] hour is adorned with an assembly of joys.
Hosts of kinnaras [pour out] trills(?), flocks of parrots sing,
hosts of cuckoos sweetly and charmingly — [their] fifth note (panchama) reigns. *(Refrain)*[^02-47]
The fresh flowers have opened in the kunjas; there swarm upon swarm of bees
hum so charmingly, as if sweet instruments were playing.
Shadja, pairs [of voices], a lovely manner — [the peacocks] call out "dhi-dhi-kiti dhilanga";
the peacocks dance — seeing [them], the celestial dancing girls are ashamed.[^02-48]
The swans cry "Sadhu!" — hearing [it], the krauncha cranes lose their composure;
in the guise of sprouts there is a thrill of rapture: the earth, laden with creepers, does not let [it] go (?).[^02-49]
Wonderful! Intoxicated with that prema, many (hundreds of) rows of doves shine;
in the guise of the sound "ghu-ghu" [their] hunkara rumbles deep.
The wind, the robber of night's adornment, shakes the leaves with boundless delight;
in the guise of flowers it joyfully gives away corals and pearls.
On the grass fall drops of dew — as if tears of bliss were flowing.
Narahari says: [such is] the incomparable [dawn] in the town of Nadia on earth.[^02-50]

*Modaka-chhandah*

**2.15a.** At the close of night the lovely birds and the rest, in this way,
for the happiness of [their] Master, intoxicated with rasa, display their gifts.
Having woken, [He] is not awake: [still] He stays a little in sleep
on the lovely bed — Gaura, the thief of hearts.
There a skilful parrot, raised by the Lord's servant,
shines in a new cage — charming in nature, sedate.
With supreme joy in its heart, taking Narahari along,
it carefully wakes [the Lord], watching [His] play.[^02-51]

*Raga bhairava*

**2.16a.** Wake up, life of the world, new moon of the town of Nadia, O!
Auspicious King Madana, lovely in form [the colour] of gorochana,
full of rasa, overcome by rasa, ornament of the rasikas, root of rasa, O! *(Refrain)*[^02-52]
Most excellent beauty with teeth [like] kunda, with a lovely, soft, delight-giving face,
with lovely restless eyes — a snare for the eyes and hearts of people, O!
On the lovely chest a sweet garland, swaying; delight of women,
stealing [their] composure, [Yourself] the abode of composure, crushing Kama(?), O![^02-53]
Beauty-giving curling hair, incomparable charming dress;
in the lake of the devotees' hearts [You] rival a golden lotus, O!
Lion-necked, with pure ears, golden, marked with a tilaka of sandal,
wearing cloud-coloured cloth, king of dancers, delighting the host of loved ones, O!
Charming temple of jewel-like virtues, whose gait defeats the elephant, grateful one,
[You] whose feet, which break [the bonds of] worldly existence, are worshipped by the gods, O!
Narahari [says]: O Beloved, [here is] the word of [my] heart — what shall I say? nothing can be expressed, —
today, seeing You asleep, anxiety seizes me, O![^02-54]

*Again raga yatharaga*

**2.17a.** Leave Your bed, O Gaura, abode of virtues!
The moon has grown pale, the watches of the night have passed.
The eastern quarter, O friend, has forgotten everything
and in anuraga has robed herself in red.[^02-55]
The kumuda, the bee's dwelling, has closed;
[the bee] goes to the opened lotus.
The chakravaki rejoices together with her mate;
Narahari, seeing [this], laughs — so much fun!

*Again raga yatharaga*

**2.18a.** The night has gone, the moon's pride is far away;
in great sorrow the chakora pines.[^02-56]
Ashamed at heart over their husband's humiliation,
the stars have hidden in the forest of the sky.
The people of Nadia have already woken —
that is why I say: leave Your bed, Gora!
If You do not believe me,
then ask Narahari.

*Again raga yatharaga*

**2.19a.** Wake up, wake up, O Gora, [my] life,
thief of the hearts and eyes of the people of the world!
I do not know what has so absorbed You
that You sleep [even] at the hour of dawn.[^02-57]
Open Your eyes and see: the night is over,
all these neighbours have woken;
leaving sorrow behind, floating in an ocean of happiness,
they laugh [at You] in so many ways.
And I say more: in this town of Nadia
everyone praises [You] in all sorts of ways;
they come running to Your house —
does this not make You feel at all ashamed?
What strange laziness You have taken on:
morning comes, and You cannot get up!
Tell me, then, what work do You do at night?
You are so accomplished — why are You like this?
The peacock and peahen stay apart,
neither comes near the other;
dispirited, they sit in the trees:
if You do not look [at them], they do not dance.
The bee and his mate in the lovely kunja
have forgotten to settle on the clusters of flowers;
"To whom shall we sing?" they say, and do not hum,
but wander through the forest as if in despair.
The chakora, [seeing] in the moon of the sky a likeness of the moon of Your face,
had become attached to the moon of the heavens;
[now] it has grown pale — and, fallen into this confusion,
[the chakora] weeps in great sorrow: "What will become of me?"[^02-58]
Make it happy, O heap of happiness:
get up, come out into the courtyard and stand there,
or else my heart is heavy:
the blame of Narahari will not wash away.[^02-59]

*Again raga yatharaga*

**2.20a.** Wake up, wake up, O moon-Gaura! How long will You sleep — the night is over.
Leaving their homes, Your associates have quickly come and surrounded the courtyard.
I see no one like all of them: [they are] like chakora birds without the moon.
So quickly leave Your bed, show Yourself [to them] and satisfy [their] thirsting eyes.
What shall I say? [O You whose] deeds are lovely — in vain(?) [they] sit here in silence.
Let [them] hear even once the words of [Your] moon-like lips, and erase the line [of grief] from [their] hearts.
With restless minds [they] look all around; I have understood: [they] are utterly exhausted.
Embrace [them] and make [their] bodies stronger(?), O Lord of Narahari's life![^02-60]

*Again raga yatharaga*

**2.21a.** The night is over — the men and women, one and all, have come back to life (lit. "have found life").
Thinking of the host of Your lovely deeds, no one has a mind for household work.
In great haste they have all come to You to be satisfied [with the sight of You] —
and You do not wake: this is very hard. I do not know what happiness there is in [Your] sleep!
All the brahmanas of Nadia, about their own business, have hurried to the bank of the Suradhuni;
they laugh among themselves: "Look, Nimai Pandita is [still] lying there!"
So I say: leave Your bed, perform the morning rites, O Gora, jewel of virtues!
Otherwise everyone will sing of Your disrepute — and Narahari, hearing it, will be ashamed.[^02-61]

*Again raga yatharaga*

**2.22a.** Get up, get up! What a wondrous sleep You are sleeping today, O Clever One!
Never have I seen such a habit in You — how much longer must I reason with You?
At such an hour, so much laziness — what happiness [is in it]? People laugh at Your doings.
Were [things] as before, now there would be no need to wake [You] — [You would already] be running away out of shame.
In the same way Your Gadadhara, Narahari and all the others lie [asleep].
They have nothing to fear — so that is well; otherwise they would have run off, leaving You behind.[^02-62]
What shall I say? You lie with Your dear ones — what is lost by that?
[But] if it grows very late, afterwards You will not be able to blame Narahari for anything.[^02-63]

*Again raga yatharaga*

**2.23a.** Wake up, thief of people's hearts, best of the clever, beautiful One who sports in the town of Nadia!
Radha is the crest-jewel of women, full of rasa: You are the delight of the treasure of Her heart.
What shall I say? Again and again [I tell You]: the night has turned to dawn.
What laziness is this? I understand nothing — many doubts [remain] in my heart. *(Refrain)*[^02-64]
When You hear of the lovely deeds and virtues [of the people] of Vraja, neither food nor sleep pleases You;
while telling [of them], You do not distinguish(?) day from night — so how can sleep please You now?
The matchless sankirtana, the root of happiness, You always hold dearer than life;
without it a moment is for You like a kalpa. At this, Narahari's mind is bewildered.[^02-65]

*Manjumukhi-chhandah*

**2.24a.** The very skilful parrot spoke much in this way.
Gaura in His sleep heard a little, intoxicated with rasa.
Seeing that the bed was grieved, He who gives joy to all
stretched a little — and remained [lying]: He cannot leave [it].[^02-66]

*Raga yatharaga*

**2.25a.** Languid and limp, the Lord, crest-jewel of rasikas, speaks, as if in a dream, words full of rasa:
"Bereft of the rasa of seeing Radha-ramana, [My] life is burning, burning away in fever.[^02-67]
Listen, Gauri, Haridasa, Dhananjaya, Sanjaya, Vijaya, Mukunda, Murari,
Madhava, Vasudeva, Purushottama, Shridhara, Krishnadasa who gives joy,
Shrinidhi, Madhusudana, Vakreshvara, Satyaraja, wise Kavichandra,
Shankara, Garuda, Bhagavata, Nandana, Chandrashekhara, profound Saranga,
Shuklambara, Yadunatha, Nakula, Vanamali, Mahesha, Shrinidhi — abode of virtues![^02-68]
Providence, most kind, understanding My heart, has given Me unceasing company with all of you.
Therefore, accepting My humble request — that [narrative] full of the rasa of the lovely deeds of the people of Vraja —
sing it with brilliance in sweet ragas, and remove this cruel burning of [My] heart.[^02-69]
How much more shall I reveal what is hidden in My heart? These mighty wicked enemies have made [Me] their subject.
I took this body in vain, I have understood nothing; I have become [one wandering] without a path, [poor] in the fullness of prema(?).[^02-70]
Again, joining My palms, I say: all of you together, gladly fulfil the heart's desire of your own [dear one]:
birth after birth, without obstruction, may I be in this world a servant of the lotus feet of the Lord of the gopis."[^02-71]
Having spoken such words, the king of the twice-born, sinking again a little into sleep, fell silent.
Narahari is bewildered: he cannot in the least describe [these] matchless lovely deeds, rare even for the gods.[^02-72]

*Chatushpadi-chhandah*

**2.26a.** Who can tell [this] lovely lila to its end?
The youthful Gaura remained asleep in [His] house.
Narahari constantly longs:
"When shall I behold the play of the close of night?"

*Thus [ends] the second kirana of the Sri Sri Gaura-charita-chintamani, called "The Description of the Shayana-vilasa", in the description of the deeds of the nishanta period.*[^02-73]

[^02-1]: The title of the kirana is taken from its colophon (at the end of the kirana, after 2.26): "…niśānta-kālīya-caritra-varṇane śayana-vilāsa-varṇanaṁ nāma dvitīyaḥ kiraṇaḥ" — "the second kirana, called 'The Description of the Shayana-vilasa', in the description of the deeds of the nishanta period". Shayana-vilasa — the pastime in which the Lord reposes on His bed. Nishanta (lit. "the close of night") — the first of the eight periods of the ashtakaliya-lila, the last hours of the night before dawn (cf. 1.59, 1.61). The subheading "Now — in the description of the deeds of the eight periods — first of all.

[^02-2]: "Full" — the edition has *pūrā se*, in quotation marks (a doubtful place). "Delighter" — *rañjaka*; "rays" — *kara* (also "hands"): the moon wiped away the gloom with his ray-hands.

[^02-3]: Some associates spend the night by the Lord's house; others, by His command, stay in their own homes — and it is they whom separation torments.

[^02-4]: Yatharaga ("to a suitable raga") — a marking of the edition: no raga is specified.

[^02-5]: "Half a moment" — *tila ādha*, lit. "half a sesame seed" (the smallest fraction of time). *kalapa alapa kariyā bāse* — lit. "they consider the small a kalpa"; a kalpa is a day of Brahma, an immense span.

[^02-6]: A play on words: *niśānta* — "the end of night" and "dwelling, house" (probably also "tranquil"). *antara-rahita* — "without interval" ("never apart"; it can also be understood as "without separation [from Him]"). The first three lines in the edition are the refrain (*dhru*).

[^02-7]: "Cloud" — *ghana*; together with "Narahari" a hint at the author's other name, Ghanashyama. The chataka is a bird that, by poetic tradition, drinks only rainwater from the clouds. *(nāhi)* — the editor's insertion in brackets; the end of the line is unclear: *samujhata anidāna* — "(do not) understand… causeless" (or "unending").

[^02-8]: Madana (Kama, Kamadeva), the god of love, is the standard of beauty; Gaura's beauty crushes the pride of many Madanas. The Jahnavi is the Ganga. *kapura kheta* is taken as "white as camphor" (*kheta* < *śveta*).

[^02-9]: *kiratī* is taken as *kīrti*: Kirti ("Fame") personified as a maiden; in Indian poetics fame is white. The white bed is Kirti's breast, golden Gaura the necklace upon it. It may also be understood as "a Kirata girl" (of the Kirata mountain tribe).

[^02-10]: *amanda* — lit. "not dim, not feeble". Shankara is Shiva.

[^02-11]: Reading unclear: the first word of the line — *kṛta(?)*; *lakṣa-lakṣa* — "hundreds of thousands" (lakhs); *vipakṣa* — "opponents, rivals"; *randhragāḥ(?)* (the middle letters run together) — taken as "passing through the openings". Malaya is the mountain range in South India from which the cool, sandal-scented breeze blows.

[^02-12]: The bimba is a red fruit, a traditional comparison for lips. The word before *bimba* reads *cāmala(?)* — meaning unclear.

[^02-13]: Meaning unclear: *mañjutama-kramapatha-prānta suprānta*; perhaps *prānta* for *śrānta* ("weary"): bees tired from a long flight have settled on the face.

[^02-14]: *kiñcid alapa lopa* — "slightly smudged". Here (and in 2.12) Gaura's beauty is described through its effect on women — a poetic hyperbole of beauty (cf. 2.4, 2.7: Gaura is more beautiful than Madana). Gaudiya Vaishnavas, following Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati and the *Chaitanya-bhagavata* (Adi 15: in His lila the Lord did not jest with women or even look at them), reject *gaura-nagari-vada*, the worship of Gaura as an enjoying lover: Gaura is Krishna in the mood of Radha, tasting Her love, not an enjoyer.

[^02-15]: A pun: *guṇa* means both "bowstring" and "virtue". The curved ears are compared to Kama's bow. *dvayarāma* — "delightful pair"; *gurubara* is taken as "surpassing"; *karma re* — "in action, oh!". The sense of the comparison *sujana mana sama* ("like the minds of the noble") is unclear.

[^02-16]: The closed eyes are the quivers of Kama's arrows, resting after victory: the Lord's glances are the arrows. *dalanakṣi* is taken as "eyes that crush [the beauty of lotus petals]"; *lasa lobha* — "shining, captivating".

[^02-17]: Reading unclear: *mānta/mālya(?)* — taken as *mālya* ("garlands"). *vilasad-alakṣa* — "shining, countless" (or "imperceptible") signs; *asta-vyasta* — "in disarray".

[^02-18]: *cīna ambara* — "Chinese cloth", silk. Reading unclear: *śobhīnakara(?)* — taken as *śobhā-nikara* ("a mass of beauty").

[^02-19]: Reading unclear: *gatabhuja* is taken as "having laid the arm"; *ākuñca(?)* — "slightly bent"; the syllable *na* at the beginning of the last line (*datta / na asavya*) — meaning unclear. *asavya kara* — "the right hand".

[^02-20]: Kama is the very type of perfect beauty; cf. 2.4. In the edition the first three lines are the refrain (*dhru*). In the edition there is an ink blot between *jhalakata* and *janu*.

[^02-21]: Sumeru is the golden mountain at the centre of the world: here, Gaura's golden body. *bahu thora* — "much — little": the waves are now stronger, now gentler.

[^02-22]: *bila-saba* (thus, with a hyphen, in the edition) is taken as *vilasaba* ("will rest, will sport").

[^02-23]: "The lord of the gods" is Indra. Autumn is the season of a clear, cloudless sky. *manobhava-balinā* — lit. "by the might of Manobhava (Kama)"; taken as "surpassing the might of Manobhava"; meaning unclear.

[^02-24]: Hara's mountain is Kailasa, Shiva's snow-white abode. Reading unclear: *santa(?)* (*akhaṇḍa santa dadhi-piṇḍa*); *gaṅga-nira(?)* is taken as *gaṅgā-nīra* ("the waters of the Ganga").

[^02-25]: Bandhuka is a red flower; karavira — oleander; ketaki — screwpine; jati — jasmine. Reading and meaning unclear: *bandhuka(?)*; *kanakāja(?)* — perhaps *kanakābja* ("golden lotus") or *kanaka* ("champaka"); *kṛta nayanā* is taken as "[to delight] the eyes"; *jhaṭita* (next line) — meaning unclear.

[^02-26]: *mandira deve* is taken as "to the Lord (Deity) of the chamber", i.e. Gaura. Reading unclear: *urathita* is taken as "raised"; *catuṣki* as "a four-legged [frame]" (or four-posted).

[^02-27]: *rada kara* is taken as "made of ivory" (*rada* — "tusk").

[^02-28]: Numerical symbols: *veda* — "four" (after the number of the Vedas), *vasu* — "eight" (after the number of the Vasus). *vihi sama* is taken as "according to rule" (*vidhi*).

[^02-29]: The paintings depict the pastimes in the groves (kunjas) of Vraja. *bhramahārī* — "dispelling bewilderment" (or "dispelling weariness"). The Suradhuni is the Ganga; the sense of the comparison (bright, pure?) is unclear; perhaps *sura-dhani* — "[rich] as that of the gods".

[^02-30]: Manmatha is Kama. On "steal the composure of women" see note 14. Nandana is Indra's heavenly garden. "Dear" — printed *priṃ-*, a misprint for *priya-*.

[^02-31]: In His sleep Gaura recalls the "former" — Krishna's pastimes with Radha in a nikunja (bower) of Vraja — and speaks Krishna's words addressed to Radha ("crest-jewel of women"). Gaura is Krishna Himself, who has taken the mood and lustre of Radha; the pastimes of Vraja live in His heart, and at times Krishna's mood awakens in Him. Reading unclear: *niragana* — perhaps *nirakhana* ("beholding") or *nirgaṇa* ("countless").

[^02-32]: Reading and meaning unclear: *sītkṛta-pāna*; *sītkṛta* is taken as "a soft whisper, sigh"; perhaps *śītakṛta* ("refreshing").

[^02-33]: Reading and meaning unclear: *karikara sama gāna* is taken as *kari kara sama-gāna* ("having made [a graceful movement], sing together"); *karikara* may also mean "an elephant's trunk" (a traditional comparison).

[^02-34]: *narahari pa̐hu* — "Narahari's Lord", i.e. the author's Lord (the author's signature). *vinidagata* is taken as *vinidra-gata* ("having awakened").

[^02-35]: A continuation of 2.13 (cf. note 31): in His sleep Gaura sees Himself in a kunja of Vraja and speaks the words of Krishna, waking Rai (Radha) at dawn and urging Her home. Dhani ("lovely one, fortunate one") is a form of address to Radha in the padas. Krishna's words were heard by Gadadhara (last line), whom Gaudiya Vaishnavas honour as Radha in Gaura's lila (*Gaura-ganoddesha-dipika*). The first two lines in the edition are the refrain (*dhru*).

[^02-36]: "The night and the moon have shrunk" (*rajanī rajanī-kara kuñcita*) — the night is ending, the moon has dimmed. The chakravaki is a bird that, by poetic tradition, is parted from her mate for the night and reunited with him at dawn. "Beloved" — printed *premakara* ("maker of love", i.e. the sun, the beloved of the lotuses); possibly a misprint for *prabhākara* ("the sun"). The kumuda is a white night lily: at dawn it closes, shutting in the bee; *vibhaṅga* — "broken, bewildered".

[^02-37]: "Unnoticed" — *alakhita*; the first letter is indistinct. The elders (*guru-jana*) — the relatives from whom Radha hides Her meetings.

[^02-38]: "My" — printed *najhu*, probably a misprint for *majhu* ("my"). "Calmed his troubled [heart]" — *athira thira karu*, lit. "made the unsteady steady" (it may also be understood as "troubled, he collected himself").

[^02-39]: "Excellent" — *subara*; the second letter is indistinct (ব or ধ).

[^02-40]: The Milk Ocean is the white bed (cf. 2.4); Sumeru (the golden mountain) is Gaura's golden body. Reading and sense unclear: *suleha-maṇḍita* — understood as "adorned with unguents" (*leha*, *lepa*); possibly *neha* ("love"). "Shattering the pride of the sun" — *caṇḍakara-mada-bhañjana* (*caṇḍakara*, "the fierce-rayed", is the sun). The edition gives the metre's name without "chhandah".

[^02-41]: Smara is Kama (cf. 2.19: Manmatha). "Softly quivers" — *mṛdu thira thira* (Bengali *thirathira*, "to tremble slightly"). Reading unclear: *sucalita* ("stirred"; the second letter looks like a fused *ল*). The end of the line is unclear: *sujana kṛta prāṇahi* — understood as "has become the life of the good"; possibly "has revived the good".

[^02-42]: All the similes concern the dark pupils within the white of the half-closed eyes: the jambu is a dark fruit; the atasi is blue flax; the kunda is white jasmine; Hara's mountain is snow-white Kailasa, the abode of Shiva; kumuda is printed *kumada*. In Indian poetics fame is white. In poetics madhura-rasa is dark (shyama) in colour; the ruby throne is probably the eyes slightly reddened by sleep.

[^02-43]: Reading unclear: *su?alita* (a letter is damaged) — probably *suvalita* ("slender, rounded"). *yuvati-vijaya* — understood as "conquering [the beauty of the faces of] young maidens" (in poetry maidens' faces are compared to the moon); cf. note 14. The bimba is a red fruit; *kadana* — "tormenting, shaming"; *kiñcid-amilana* — "slightly parted".

[^02-44]: A manini is a heroine in mana (jealous pique); in the padas, Radha. The poet likens the sleeping Gaura's hands, joined on His chest, to Krishna entreating Radha to give up Her mana (*parihāra*, "a plea for forgiveness"): in His sleep Gaura is still in the mood of Krishna (cf. 2.13–2.14, note 31).

[^02-45]: The celestial river (*surasarit*) is the Ganga. Reading and sense unclear: *patrī sanidha* — understood as "birds" (*patrī*) and possibly "near" (*sannidhi*). *nidharaka* — "without fear"; *karakeṣu* — Sanskrit "on the pomegranate trees" (*karaka*, pomegranate). Cocks — *tāmracūḍa* ("red-crested"). The nipa is the kadamba. The khanjana is a wagtail.

[^02-46]: Aprakrita — transcendental, not belonging to material nature; prakrita — mundane. The residents of Navadvipa-dhama, even its insects, plants and grass, are eternal associates of the Lord, though outwardly they resemble ordinary beings.

[^02-47]: With this stanza the edition starts numbering from 14 again: after 2.20 come the numbers 14, 15, 16, 17, 18…; the number 14 is clearly legible. The edition's numbering is kept, and the repetition is marked with the letter "a" (2.14a–2.18a). Reading unclear: *hulaha-tāna* (or *gulaha-*); *tāna* — a musical run, a trill. Kinnaras are celestial singers. Panchama is the fifth note of the scale, by tradition the voice of the cuckoo. The first three lines are the refrain (*dhru*).

[^02-48]: Shadja is the first note of the scale, by tradition the voice of the peacock. *yugmaka* — "pairs"; *sudhaṅga* — understood as "a lovely manner". *ughaṭata dhidhikiṭi dhilaṅga* — "call out 'dhi-dhi-kiti dhilanga'": syllables of the mridanga's drum rhythm, recited in dance. The celestial dancing girls (*sura-nartakī*) are the apsaras.

[^02-49]: "Sadhu!" — "Excellent!". The krauncha is a crane. The sprouts that have pushed up out of the earth are like hairs standing on end in rapture (*pulaka*). Reading and sense unclear: *bhūmi nadi tāje* — understood as *nahi tyāje* ("does not let go").

[^02-50]: "Many (hundreds of)" — printed *baṃ* (probably *bahu*, "many"), with the editor's correction *(śata)*, "hundreds", in brackets. Hunkara is a rumbling cry of ecstasy; "ghu-ghu" is the cooing of doves. "The robber of night's adornment" — *niśa siṅgāra hāra* (the sense is conjectural); the corals and pearls are red and white flowers. *rījha* — "delight". *mahimājhe* — understood as *mahi-mājhe* ("on earth"); possibly "[such is] the glory (*mahimā*)".

[^02-51]: "Skilful" — *sughara*. "Taking Narahari along" — the author's signature woven into the narrative: together with the parrot he wakes the Lord (possibly Narahari Sarakara, an associate of the Lord, is meant — the text does not make it clear). It is probably the same parrot that wakes Him — its words seem to be heard in 2.17a–2.18a.

[^02-52]: "King Madana" (*madana-bhūpa*) is Kama, the king of love. Gorochana is a bright yellow pigment (Gaura's golden colour). "Delight-giving" — *raṅgada*; the reading is indistinct. The first three lines are the refrain (*dhru*).

[^02-53]: "Delight of women, stealing [their] composure" (*lalanābhirāma*, *dhṛti-bhara-hara*) — cf. 2.12 and note 14. Reading and sense unclear: *kāma dalata śanda* — understood as "crushing Kama".

[^02-54]: "Cloud-coloured cloth" (*meghāmbara*) — dark blue. The gods — *vṛndāraka*. "Anxiety" — *danda* (< *dvandva*: "doubt, anxiety"): the Lord is still asleep, though it is time to rise.

[^02-55]: "Again raga yatharaga" — *punar-yathārāga* in the edition. *sakhi* is understood as the address "O friend" (possibly: "the eastern quarter has forgotten all her friends"). A play on words: *anurāga* is both "love" and "redness": the east has reddened at dawn.

[^02-56]: The chakora is a bird that, by poetic tradition, feeds on moonlight. Reading unclear: the last word of the line is *jhiye(?)* (probably *jhure*, "pines"). The stars (nakshatras) are the wives of the moon (Chandra). The stanza ends.

[^02-57]: Here the edition continues the repeated numbering of kirana 2 (see note 47): after 2.18a come the numbers 19–26; they are marked with the letter "a" (2.19a–2.26a). According to 2.24a, these words (2.16a–2.23a) are spoken by the parrot who wakes the Lord together with Narahari (cf. 2.15a, note 51). Stanzas 2.19a–2.22a are in Bengali (not Vrajabuli). *bhorā* — "absorbed, intoxicated"; *biyāna bele* — "at the hour of dawn".

[^02-58]: The chakora is a bird said in poetic tradition to feed on moonlight. While Gaura's face is hidden in sleep, the chakora contents itself with the moon of the sky as a likeness of His face; at dawn the moon has grown pale, and the bird is in confusion (*dhānda*).

[^02-59]: Lit. "the fault of Narahari, [even if] washed, will not go away": if the Lord does not rise, the blame will fall on the one who was to wake Him.

[^02-60]: Reading unclear: *vṛthā(?)* ("in vain"; the first letter looks like র or ব with a hook beneath; possibly *kathā*: "what shall I say of the lovely deeds"). *sudhānātha* — "lord of nectar", the moon; "moon-like lips" — Gaura's lips. *rekhā* — "line", understood as a trace of grief. *khiṇa* (*kṣīṇa*) — "exhausted". Reading unclear: *puṣṭa(?)tara kuri* (in print it looks like *hupa tara*) — understood as "make [their bodies] stronger"; *deha* may also be taken as the imperative "give". "Lord of Narahari's life" — the author's signature.

[^02-61]: Nimai Pandita — the Lord's name in Navadvipa, where He was a teacher. The Suradhuni is the Ganga; at dawn the brahmanas go there to bathe and perform their morning rites. *gorā guṇera maṇi* — "Gora, jewel of virtues".

[^02-62]: "As before" — probably as in Vraja, where at dawn Krishna hurried to leave the kunja so as not to be seen (cf. 2.14 and note 35). Gadadhara is Radha in Gaura's lila (note 35); Narahari Sarakara, the Lord's associate, is according to the *Gaura-ganoddesha-dipika* Madhumati, a friend of Radha. In Vraja they would have run away at dawn, leaving Krishna; in Navadvipa they have nothing to fear.

[^02-63]: *kisera yābe* — "what is lost [by it]". *belādhika* — "when the day has [too far] advanced". The sense of the last line: Narahari has given warning, and there will be nothing to blame him for.

[^02-64]: The first four lines in the edition are the refrain. Gaura is called the beloved of Radha: He is Krishna Himself (cf. note 31). *tākara hṛdaya-ratana-rucikārī* — understood as "giving delight to the treasure of Her heart"; possibly "the lovely treasure of Her heart".

[^02-65]: Reading unclear: *bāchahe(?)* — understood as "distinguish". Sankirtana — the congregational singing of the Lord's names and pastimes, held each night in the house of Srivasa (cf. 1.61). A kalpa is a day of Brahma, an immense span of time (cf. note 5).

[^02-66]: The parrot is the one raised by the Lord's servant (2.15a). *mori* — "stretching". "The bed was grieved" — because the Lord was about to leave it.

[^02-67]: Radha-ramana ("the delight of Radha") is Krishna. Whereas in 2.13–2.14 Gaura in His sleep spoke the words of Krishna, here He speaks in the mood of Radha separated from Krishna: Gaura is Krishna who has accepted Radha's mood in order to taste Her love (cf. notes 14, 31). *jara jara jīu jīu jari yāta* — lit. "the life, all in fever, burns away".

[^02-68]: A list of the Lord's associates in Navadvipa; the identifications are tentative: Gauri — probably Gauridasa Pandita (possibly the address "O Gauri"); Haridasa; Dhananjaya Pandita; Sanjaya (Mukunda-Sanjaya, in whose house Nimai taught); Vijaya (Vijaya Dasa the copyist?); Mukunda Datta; Murari Gupta; Madhava and Vasudeva (Ghosha); Shridhara (the seller of banana-leaf cups); Shrinidhi; Vakreshvara Pandita; Satyaraja Khan; Kavichandra; Shankara Pandita; Garuda Pandita; Bhagavata Acharya; Nandana Acharya; Chandrashekhara Acharya; Saranga (Sharnga Murari?); Shuklambara Brahmachari; Yadunatha; Nakula Brahmachari; Vanamali Pandita; Mahesha Pandita. The name Shrinidhi is repeated in the list (lines 5 and 7) — so in the edition.

[^02-69]: "Providence" (*vihi*, *vidhi*) — the Creator, destiny. *parabhāga* — "excellence, brilliance": to sing with the highest art. Cf. CC Antya: in separation from Krishna, Mahaprabhu was consoled with songs and verses by Svarupa Damodara and Ramananda Raya.

[^02-70]: "Wicked enemies" (*khala ripu*) — the six inner enemies (the evil qualities of the mind). These are words of humility (*dainya*) spoken by the Lord in the mood of a devotee; cf. the *Shikshashtaka* (CC Antya 20). Reading and sense unclear: *prema-bharāti* — understood as "[poor] in the fullness of prema"; possibly "in an excess of prema".

[^02-71]: *sukha-sañe* — "gladly". The Lord of the gopis (*gopī-pati*) is Krishna. Cf. the *Shikshashtaka* (CC Antya 20): the prayer for service to Krishna's feet birth after birth.

[^02-72]: "King of the twice-born" (*dvija-kula-bhūpa*) — Gaura, born in a brahmana family. *dulaha* — *durlabha*, "hard to attain".

[^02-73]: "The play of the close of night" (*niśi-śeṣa-vilāsa*) — what is described next, in kirana 3 (the Lord's waking). The colophon of kirana 2: "…nishanta-kaliya-charitra-varnane shayana-vilasa-varnanam nama dvitiyah kiranah".


## Kirana 3. The Deeds of the Morning: Description of the Waking (Shayyotthana)[^03-1]

*Pratah — morning in Navadvipa: Gaura is still asleep and speaks in His sleep; the associates, hearing His words, rise from their beds and go to His bedchamber to wake Him*

*Now — the deeds of the morning period.*

*Kamala-chhandah*

**3.1.** Gaura the moon, best of rasikas, accomplished, the crown of my head —
at the close of night, in happy sleep, absorbed in rasa —
on a lovely bed, the supreme abode of beauty,
all full of rasa, languid, shines beyond compare.
[His] deeds are most lovely; matchlessly deep is [His] sleep;
as if in a dream He speaks many words — there is no end to them.
What poet could compose all those streams of nectar?
Filling their ears [with them], the associates drink [them] unceasingly.
Each leaving his own bed, in great joy,
every limb covered with abundant fresh thrills [of ecstasy],
absorbed in rasa, smiling, speaking the sweetest words,
[they] go to the Lord's bedchamber, — [says] the servant Narahari.[^03-2]

*Kamala-chhandah*

**3.2.** Gaura's associates — the greatest rasikas, excellent, the crown of the steadfast.
Who can fathom their feelings? Their hearts, [full of] fresh joy, know no rest.
Walking slowly, they all came near to the abode of bliss.
Many stand leaning against the lovely walls, — in a matchless way;
gracefully [bending], pressing their eyes to the window, with great eagerness,
they plunge into the ocean of the beauty of Gaura, shining on the bed.
At once, [as if] offering their bodies [to Him] in nirmanchhana, — the longings of the heart have no end, —
they again say something to one another, — [says] Narahari, — immersed in joyful delight.[^03-3]

*Kumari-chhandah (someone says)*[^03-4]

**3.3.** Look, look: beautiful, excellent young Gaura,
who crushes the pride of Manmatha, the thief of the hearts of young maidens![^03-5]
[He is] steady lightning, the king of gold(?), of ketaki and champaka;
[His] form surpasses thick kunkuma and the golden mountain.[^03-6]
The curves of [His] limbs are wondrous, full of rasa, abounding in grace;
adorned with a host of various jewelled ornaments, [He] steals composure.
[His] face puts to shame heaps of lotuses — a lovely, sweet radiance;
the lips subdue the [redness of the] bimba, the teeth are kunda and pearls.
Matchless are the corners of the mouth, lovely the chin — so [He] shines;
the ears adorned with earrings and flowers, the charming cheeks;
the nose shines, lovely and excellent are the eyes — the delight of young maidens(?);
the brows are serpents, bees; the forehead gleams, an abode of beauty,
like the moon; bright are the sandal tilaka and the curls;
the hair — rare(?) hair; the fresh attire slightly loosened.[^03-7]
The neck with three lines, the high shoulders — exceedingly lovely;
the arms long, [like] door-bars, dispel the pride of elephants' trunks;
the chest, marked with barely visible [auspicious] signs, is very broad;
the navel is deep, the slender waist wrapped in blue cloth.
The shanks — as if the plantain trunk, [which strove to rival them], lost its pride;
the wondrously formed knees put golden caskets to shame.
The tenderest ankles and heels are more lovely still;
the soles are land-lotuses, [like] the young dawn.
The star-like toenails gleam, adorning [the feet] like jewelled mirrors.
Narahari, offering [himself to Him in] nirmanchhana, always meditates on [Him] in his heart.[^03-8]

*And another — in raga vibhasha*

**3.4.** Ah, ah! Wondrous is Gaura's form —
the world-enchanting king Manmatha.
What am I to do? I was not given countless eyes:
cruel fate has brought [me] sorrow in beholding.[^03-9]
I keep this longing, filling my heart with it,
and, like a priceless jewel, I do not reveal it.
Who fashioned such shapely curves of [His] body?
[He] is my all in all, matchless in this world.
Every day at the close of night I see [Him],
but such sleep I have never seen.
So I understand: a new sleep reigns [now].
Narahari: will [anyone] wake [Him] today in such [a sleep]?

*(And another — raga bhairava)*

**3.5.** Blessed, blessed — I count tonight blessed:
such sleep of the Lord, who thirsts for the rasa of sankirtana, I have never seen! *(Refrain)*
He, maddened by the fullness of His former mood, ceaselessly speaks of beautiful Vraja;
the lids of His eyes never close even for a moment — staying awake all night, He meets the dawn.
Now He is sunk in matchless sleep — will He wake? This is a play more wondrous still.
Wondrous is the way of [His] sleep: as if in a dream, He utters words like nectar.
The night has gone, morning has come, — and still He who sports in Nadiya does not wake.
How to fathom these deeds of Narahari's Lord? Whether this sleep will break — I cannot say.[^03-10]

*And another — raga lalita*

**3.6.** Behold the wondrous play of the Lord!
[His] graceful, deep, sweet sleep gives rise to the joy of body, mind and eyes. *(Refrain)*
He whose bodily radiance does not appear even a little in the hearts of the gods, however much they strive, —
that mass of radiance, by [our] good fortune(?), here in the chamber flashes, surpassing rows of suns.
The sages, to picture [Him] on the lovely bed of their hearts, perform sacrifices kalpa after kalpa;
unattainable to them, He is attainable to this prepared bed, — what poet could describe its fortune?
The praises of Brahma and Shiva do not delight [Him], yet now He is pleased, hearing the parrot in the new cage speak at length.
How long will Narahari's Lord stay hidden? That former way [of His] is plainly manifest.[^03-11]

*And another — raga lalita*

**3.7.** Look, gaze at Gauranga!
Who knows such sleep?
What postures of hands and feet!
Is it like this in sleep? — I never knew.
The eyes are lovely, and [what] grace in them!
Why should it be so in drowsiness?
The beauty of the moon-face has so increased —
a gentle smile, as if nectar were dripping.
Sleep or no sleep — I cannot tell;
what is in my mind — I cannot say.
Narahari: how much can one say of this?
I think it will be hard to wake [Him].

*And another — raga lalita*

**3.8.** Listen, listen, oh! I understand nothing: by what rasa is He [so] absorbed?
The night is over, and still the world-enchanting Gora sleeps(?).
And look: Gadadhara, his eyes fixed on the face of Gauranga the moon,
sits at [His] feet and, smiling, happily presses the feet.
Narahari, gazing at Gauranga, floats in an ocean of happiness
and, [bending] wondrously, says something into Gadadhara's ear.
Some, intoxicated with the rasa of Gora, sway and fall — [all are] bewildered;
some earnestly try to wake Narahari's Lord of life.[^03-12]

*Raga yatharaga*

**3.9.** At the night's sleep of Gauranga the moon
all gaze and gaze, refreshing their eyes.
With one another, their hearts full of joy,
they merrily talk of many things.
Can a poet describe this —
the matchless virtues of Gauranga?
Again and again, filling their eyes, they behold
the sweetness of the sleep of Narahari's Lord.

*Raga yatharaga*

**3.10.** Beautiful young Gaura still lies [asleep].
The sun has come to the east, people have woken and gone [about their tasks]; night has turned to dawn. *(Refrain)*
Someone, wholly absorbed, recites the sweetest verses, stanza after stanza, in a most wondrous way;
someone, together with many instruments, beautifully sings songs of the Lord's loving deeds;
someone composes lovely [verses]; someone, as is due, loudly utters the Name;
someone, intoxicated with bhakti-rasa out of humility(?), — cannot utter words clearly(?).
The cows low, full [of milk], anxious to suckle their calves;
hearing their voices, the Lord at once awoke and sat up on the bed.
Then, stretching, covering both eyes with His lovely hands, He yawned a little;
leaving the chamber, He goes with a restless heart and keeps asking(?) for the milking rope.
Seeing this, Gauridasa and the others, one after another, joyfully call [Him] by His former name.
Narahari says: wondrous is this lovely deed — is it deep sleep, or a play of prema?[^03-13]

*Raga yatharaga*

**3.11.** Gaurachandra shines — the jewel of virtues.
He reveals a wondrous former mood, — His heart is joyful, and again shyness arises. *(Refrain)*
Smiling softly, softly, He glances(?) — matchless is [His] grace(?);
He speaks words full of supreme nectar, and with a hundred stratagems hides [them].
The lotus eyes are motionless(?); greatly abashed, He stands with head bowed, looking at the ground.
He steals the hearts of His dear ones; ever-new beauty — more than can be described.
The loosened lovely hair, the attire, the garments, the garland slip as He gently walks;
quickly He sat on the seat (padapitha) — Narahari's sight, beholding [this], bore its fruit.[^03-14]

*Raga yatharaga*

**3.12.** Behold the wondrous Gaurachandra!
On a sparkling, lovely seat of jewels shines the matchless king Manmatha.
[His] beautiful body puts to shame the peak of the mountain of the gods; [His] mighty radiance has filled the three worlds.
He rises unceasingly in the hearts of His own people, shattering the pride of millions of suns and moons.[^03-15]
The face in which a soft, soft smile shines is lovely — a grove of blossoming lotuses cannot equal it.
In deep drowsiness [His] reddish eyes sway, heavy with sleep — [and that glance] destroys all the shame and fear of young maidens.[^03-16]
From the loosened hair kunda [flowers] fall — as if stars, leaving the sky, were falling to the earth.
What poet could describe this most wondrous grace? Narahari offers [himself] in nirmanchhana again and again.[^03-17]

*Prabhakara-chhandah*

**3.13.** The beautiful [Gaura] sat down on the seat (padapitha),
like Purandara in the midst of Amaravati.
When will Narahari behold Gaurachandra,
and [He] wipe away all the darkness and burning of his heart?[^03-18]

*Thus [ends] the third kirana of the Shrimad Gaura-charita-chintamani, called "The Description of the Waking (Shayyotthana)", in the description of the deeds of the morning period.*[^03-19]

[^03-1]: The name of the kirana is taken from its colophon: "…pratah-kaliya-charitra-varnane shayyotthana-varnanam nama tritiyah kiranah" — "the third kirana, called 'The Description of the Waking (lit. rising from the bed)', in the description of the deeds of the morning period". Pratah (morning) is the second of the eight periods of the ashtakaliya-lila (cf. 1.59, 1.61). The subheading "Now — the deeds of the morning period"

[^03-2]: Kamala is the name of the metre. *sughara* — "accomplished"; *śira mora* — "the crown of my head". For Gaura's words in His sleep see 2.25a. "Compose" — in print *viracana* ("composition"), probably for *viracaba*. The subject of the last lines is the associates; "the servant Narahari" is the author's signature (perhaps he too goes with them, cf. 2.15a).

[^03-3]: "Stand leaning against the walls" — *ṭhāṭi … bhīta kṛta avalamba* (*bhīta* < *bhitti*, "wall"). Nirmanchhana is the rite of waving a lamp or other offering before a revered person to ward off misfortune; "to offer oneself in nirmanchhana" is to give oneself wholly out of love. In the edition the fifth line ends with "॥" instead of "।".

[^03-4]: "Someone says" — in the edition in brackets *(ko 'py āha)*; the headings of 3.4–3.8 then begin with *ko 'pi ca* — "and another [says / sings]": each stanza is spoken by one of the associates gazing at the sleeping Lord.

[^03-5]: Here and below (3.3: "steals composure", "the delight of young maidens") Gaura's beauty is described through its effect on women — a poetic hyperbole of beauty (cf. "who crushes the pride of Manmatha", i.e. Kama). Gaudiya Vaishnavas, following Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati and the *Chaitanya-bhagavata* (Adi 15: in His lila the Lord did not jest with women or even look at them), reject *gaura-nagari-vada*, the worship of Gaura as an enjoying lover: Gaura is Krishna in the mood of Radha, tasting Her love, not an enjoyer.

[^03-6]: "Steady lightning" (*vidyut-thira*) is a usual comparison for Gaura's golden body. Reading unclear: *śoṇa* (distinct; "red") is taken as *sona* ("gold"). Ketaki and champaka are golden-yellow flowers. *nandita* — perhaps for *nindita* ("put to shame"). The golden mountain is Sumeru.

[^03-7]: The bimba is a red fruit, the kunda a white jasmine. *sṛkkaṇi* — the corners of the mouth. Reading and meaning unclear: *taruṇi-rāma* is taken as "the delight of young maidens" (the editor marks the place as doubtful in a note); *keśa keśa-dulabha* is taken as "rare hair" (perhaps *keśa* = *ka-īśa*, Brahma and Shiva: "[a beauty] unattainable even to Brahma and Shiva"). In the edition the line "the brows … an abode of beauty" ends without punctuation.

[^03-8]: Three lines (folds) on the neck, long arms and a broad chest are marks of a great personality (mahapurusha). "Door-bars" (*argala*) are a usual comparison for long, strong arms. The plantain trunk (*rambhā*) is a traditional comparison for shanks and thighs. "Knees" — *uru-parva* (lit. "the joints of the thighs"); "ankles" — in print *gulka* (= *gulpha*). The land-lotus (*sthala-padma*) is a red flower. Signature: Narahari.

[^03-9]: The lament that the Creator did not give countless eyes with which to behold the Lord to the full is a well-known motif (cf. the gopis, who reproach the Creator for making eyelids). *mari mari* is an exclamation of wonder. The ragas vibhasha (in print *vibhāṣa*) and lalita are morning ragas.

[^03-10]: In the edition the heading is in brackets; with a mark the editor notes that this pada is missing in one of the manuscripts. *saṅkīrtana-rasa-lampaṭa* — "who thirsts for the rasa of sankirtana". "Former mood" — the mood of the Vraja lila: Gaura is Krishna, who has come to taste Radha's love; whole nights He speaks of Vraja and does not close His eyes. "He who sports in Nadiya" — *nadīyā-vihārī*. "Break" — *bhāgaba*. The first two lines are the refrain (*dhru*).

[^03-11]: Reading and meaning unclear: *sukṛti* is taken as "by good fortune" (perhaps "a fair creation"). A pun: *kalapayite* ("to picture, imagine") and *kalapa* ("kalpa", a world age). "This prepared bed" — *talapa parikalapana*. "Brahma and Shiva" — *vihi bhava* (*vidhi* — Brahma, *bhava* — Shiva). The parrot — cf. 2.15a. "Former way" — the way of Krishna in Vraja (cf. 3.5).

[^03-12]: Reading unclear: *ghumā?yā* ("sleeps"; the middle letter is indistinct). Gadadhara — cf. 2.14–2.16. Narahari speaking into Gadadhara's ear is the author's signature woven into the narrative (perhaps Narahari Sarkara, the Lord's associate; cf. note 51 of kirana 2). *anubandha* — "earnest effort". The last line also contains the signature: "Narahari's Lord of life" (*narahari prāṇanāthe*).

[^03-13]: "As is due" — *niyamita* (perhaps "a fixed number of times"). Reading and meaning unclear: *dainya-kṛta* (the first letter is worn away) is taken as "out of humility"; *śabada ghaṭāpaṭatara nāhi hoya* is taken as "words do not come out clearly". "The milking rope" — *chā̐dana ḍora*: the rope used to hobble a cow's legs during milking; waking to the lowing of the cows, Gaura, in the mood of Krishna the cowherd boy, sets out to milk. "Asking" — in print *nāgata*, taken as *māgata*. Gauridasa Pandita is Subala, Krishna's friend, in Krishna's lila (*Gaura-ganoddesha-dipika*); "by His former name" — probably by a name of Krishna from the Vraja lila.

[^03-14]: Reading and meaning unclear: *vadanaku?saṅga cāyata* is taken as "glances"; *anupama mahi bisāi* — meaning unclear; *agola* — perhaps for *alola* ("motionless"). Padapitha — a low seat (in 3.12 Gaura sits on it like Indra in Amaravati). The stanza begins and ends with "॥১১॥".

[^03-15]: The first line stands in the edition centred, like a refrain, but without the mark *dhru*. "A seat of jewels" — *suratana pīṭha*; this is the padapitha on which Gaura sat at the end of 3.11. "King Manmatha" (*manamatha-bhūpa*) — Gaura, more beautiful than Manmatha (Kama) himself. "Puts to shame the peak of the mountain of the gods" — *suragiri-śikhara-dara-hara*, lit. "taking away [the pride?] of the peak of the mountain of the gods" (Sumeru): Gaura's golden body is compared to the golden mountain.

[^03-16]: *ḍhulu ḍhulata* — "sway, droop [with sleep]". Here too Gaura's beauty is described through its effect on women — see note 5 on the position of the Gaudiya Vaishnavas (*gaura-nagari-vada*).

[^03-17]: Kunda — white jasmine woven into the hair. Nirmanchhana — see note 3. Signature: Narahari.

[^03-18]: Prabhakara is the name of the metre. Purandara is Indra, king of the gods; Amaravati is his capital. Padapitha — see note 14. Signature: Narahari.

[^03-19]: Kirana 3 consists of stanzas 3.1–3.13.


## Kirana 4. The Deeds of the Morning: Description of [Gaura] Surrounded by Rows of Devotees, and Other Matters[^04-1]

*Morning in Navadvipa: Gaura, now awake, sits in the courtyard of Shachi's house, surrounded by countless associates, like the golden mountain Sumeru by the gods; the jubilation of the devotees; Nityananda, intoxicated with love for His younger Brother, gazes at Gaura; the beauty of Nityananda*

*Again, in the morning period — the description of the beauty and so on of [Gaura], seated in His own courtyard.*

*Chaturbhangi(?)-chhandah*

**4.1.** Today, [when] the night has ended, … Gaura(?) … in joyful play(?) … — in fine order; in the midst of it shines a vast assembly of associates:[^04-2]
Sri Gadadhara Pandita, Shrinivasa, Haridasa, Raghunandana, Mukunda, Narahari, Gadadhara, Pundarika Vidyanidhi, Shrigarbha, Vijaya — [like] a splendour of sun rays;
Shriman Pandita, Hiranya, Jagadisha, Vakreshvara, Sri Gauridasa Pandita, Vasudeva Dattacharya(?), Bhagavata, Sanjaya, Dhananjaya, Jagannatha — renowned in the world;
Sri Chandrashekhara, Purandara, Sadashiva, Shriman Sena, Shuklambara, Shridhara, Sri Krishnadasa, Bhagavan Pandita, Purushottama, Murari Gupta — delighted with abundant prema;
Sri Vanamali Pandita, Garuda, Buddhimanta Khan, Govinda, Gangadasa, Nandana, Narayana, Shivananda, Nandanacharya, Ratna(?), Sri Mukunda Datta — bringers of blessing;
Sri Satyaraja, Ramananda, Rama, Vidyananda, Shankara, Srila Yadunatha, Vaninatha Mishra(?), Madhusudana, Jagannatha Acharya — devoted to serving Gaura;
Srila Kavichandra, Shashthivara, Jagannatha Mishra, Gopikanta, Mahesha Pandita, Govinda, Madhava, Vasudeva, Ishana, Gopala, Narada — charming [in their] service;
Shrinatha, Ramadasa, Sri Shubhananda, Krishnadasa, Shrinidhi, Sri Jagannatha Dasa, Kashishvara, Sri Gopinatha and others — of matchless beauty, says Ghanashyama.[^04-3]

*Raga lalita*

**4.2.** Today in the house of Sri Shachi there is great happiness:
on a matchless seat (padapitha) shines beautiful Gaurachandra, king of the twice-born. *(Refrain)*
Around the Lord is a circle of dear associates, most wondrous and lovely,
like Mount Sumeru encircled by the gods: even Shesha cannot describe [this] beauty.[^04-4]
Someone, leaning his hand on another's hand, stands like a painted doll;
someone's cloth slips down — he does not set it right: what feeling he has cannot be grasped.
Someone, with a start, has left his bed and come — his eyes droop in deep drowsiness.
Says Narahari: [he] is like a bee drunk on the honey of that lotus face.[^04-5]

*Raga yatharaga*

**4.3.** Today is a joyful dawn in Shachi's courtyard — unbroken love, fresh delight in many ways!
Someone comes and goes, someone sings the wondrous raga lalita, [someone] dances, drunk with rasa;
someone, [leaning] to another's ear, softly says [something] — and, laughing and laughing, falls: he cannot hold his body up;
someone, seizing another, embraces [him], and someone bows at another's feet.
Someone asks another whether the night passed well; someone says: "Now [all] is well — [we] see the beautiful Lord!"
Someone says to another: "Blessed are you, blessed are you! Sorrow will vanish at the touch of the breeze [wafting] from your body";
someone recites new verses, prose and so on; someone, crying aloud(?), holds a blade of grass between his teeth.
The associates are countless — like a surging ocean: how can Narahari describe this with a single mouth?[^04-6]

*Raga yatharaga*

**4.4.** What shall I say of today's wondrous play?
In the midst of the spacious courtyard is Gaurahari; [around Him] His dear associates shine unceasingly. *(Refrain)*
Is [this] a clear sky without stars, where a host of moons shines brightly?
Surely they will destroy the dreadful, impenetrable darkness of the world's threefold misery.
Is [this] an arena that removes the burden of the fear of samsara — a lovely gathering of mighty wrestlers?
Surely, defeating the invincible asuras who have turned away from the Lord's feet, they will accomplish their task.
Is [this] a field without a fence, where blossoming wish-fulfilling trees have appeared?
Says Narahari: surely they will distribute matchless, priceless fruits — and no one will be left deprived.[^04-7]

*Trivikrama-chhandah*

**4.5.** In the lovely courtyard, laid out spaciously, countless people have gathered — and still there is much [room]: [such is] the inconceivable power, the beauty of [His] manifestation(?).
Gaura's beauty is a vast, dense cloud, unceasingly pouring down the lovely rasa of bliss(?), producing abundant joy in the rasikas, ever incomparable.
How many kinds of feelings arise [in them]! Unable to keep their composure, greatly intoxicated, like an irresistible elephant, they cherish various longings — who can describe [them]?
The hypocrite Narahari, fiercest of the fierce, like a bull without [the herdsman's] stick — what can he say? He only longs greatly to hold that lovely time in meditation.[^04-8]

*Sudhamukhi-chhandah*

**4.6.** The terrace of Shachi's courtyard; in its midst — the beloved [associates].
[The Lord] plays in great delight, shattering people's composure.[^04-9]

*Chitra-chhandah*

**4.7.** The Lord, Srila Nityananda the moon, root of bliss, dispeller of many doubts, lotus-eyed, worshipped by hosts of gods, eminent, steadfast, renowned —
in the midst of the circle of dear devotees, gazing at the moon of the face of Gaura-Govinda, in utmost rapture softly, softly laughs, overjoyed.
A cloud of joy, an auspicious abode — His love-filled body is full of fresh thrills [of ecstasy]; wondrous are [His] movements, He waves His lovely arms.
He shines and trembles, like a figure of the purest gold; [His] form sways with deep anuraga — how could the insignificant fool Ghanashyama describe [it]?[^04-10]

*Belavali*

**4.8.** Nityananda rejoices in His heart.
Gazing at His younger Brother, He forgets everything and plunges into that ocean of beauty. *(Refrain)*
In His mind He reflects: "Before, I saw a wondrous dark body,
[but] Gaura's golden body steals the heart even more; how am I to understand [this]? Therefore this is a hidden rasa.
This is most rare; now, somehow propitiating [the Creator], I shall ask a boon(?):
may there never be separation — may this [form] always remain as if fastened to my eyes."
So many such hopes arise in [His] heart — the way of prema is matchless, boundless.
Narahari's Lord again and again irresistibly longs from the Creator for a body made wholly of eyes.[^04-11]

*Veloara*

**4.9.** Intoxicated with His Brother's mood, the son of Padmavati stands motionless, deeply absorbed.
Again and again He trembles like a garland of lightning; His lovely body shines with hairs standing on end.
The necklace slips from His chest; His lovely hair, ornaments, clothing — He does not set them right:
surely the great golden-hued thief, unseen, swiftly steals everything away.
How much joy arises in His heart! Tears stream in torrents from His beautiful eyes —
surely a greedy chakora, having drunk too much of the nectar of that moon face, pours [it] out again.
Rising on the tips of His toes, He stands and lifts His incomparable arms —
says Ghanashyama: as if a golden mountain, leaving the earth, were going up into the sky.[^04-12]

*Veloara*

**4.10.** Wondrous is this prema — may I be offered up to it!
His heart overflows, His body is unsteady, His gait faltering — His feet cannot hold [Him] up.
Seeing the beautiful, charming [Gaura] from afar, He came near [Him] swiftly;
utterly overcome, not in control [of Himself], not [setting right] His cloth, He placed His left hand on His shoulder.
Gazing at the moon of Gaurachandra's face, He laughs again and again, softly, softly — His lips shine.
Matchless is the grace, vast the beauty — neither Shesha nor Sharada can describe even a little [of it].
Whoever hopes to attain Gaura's pure lotus feet without this Nitai —
he is the one fool in the three worlds: all [his efforts] are surely in vain, says Narahari Dasa.[^04-13]

*Raga Veloara*

**4.11.** Nitai-chand, king of rasa, shines;
[His] form surpasses a heap of kunkuma on a golden mountain lit by the dawn — a form that deceives the world(?). *(Refrain)*
The wondrous curves of [His] limbs glitter — far softer than the shirisha flower;
the fragrance of His body, which shatters the dharma and fear of young women of good families, has filled the ten directions.[^04-14]
On sweet lips a sweet soft smile; the moon face shines, pouring nectar.
The radiance of His teeth puts strings of pearls to shame; His lips are lovely, His chin steals the heart.
Pure, wide eyes — lotus petals, full of merriment — [cast glances] in so many ways;
the curved, lovely brows — Ananga's bow — put to shame serpents and rows of bees.
The forehead adorned with tilaka, earrings swaying in the ears, the nose lovely [like] Garuda's beak;
finely shaped cheeks, a proud neck, mighty arms that take away the pride of elephants' trunks.
Broad is the lovely chest that conquers the three worlds: can firm door panels be compared to it?
In the lake of the navel — hair like water-moss; the three folds shine — [like] Triveni: who, seeing [this], can keep his composure?
Who with such patience fashioned the lovely slender waist that humbles the pride of the lion?
Captivating the minds and eyes of people is the wondrous blue garment of finest silk that He wears.
Firm shanks, soft and beautiful; mighty knees appear, giving joy;
the reddish feet, the lovely rays of the nails — may [they] destroy the darkness of this Narahari's heart![^04-15]

*Again — Veloara*

**4.12.** At the dawn of night Advaita Mahashaya, [radiant] like the sun, in supreme jubilation,
ceaselessly slaps both His armpits, seeing the moon of Gaura's face shine forth.
His corpulent body, victorious in all directions(?), is most beautiful; on it fresh horripilation shines.
He wondrously walks to and fro, here and there, more intoxicated than the king of elephants.
Laughing softly, He flashes the rows of His teeth — white rays issue ceaselessly,
wondrously, — as if kunda flowers were raining in all four directions; the connoisseurs' longing and delight grow still more(?).
The tears trembling in both His eyes brim over and flow beautifully, heeding no restraint;
they sparkle like strings of pearls, — Narahari understands: the Lord is overcome [by prema](?).[^04-16]

*Raga yatharaga*

**4.13.** The Lord of Sita is immersed in great happiness.
In His mind He reflects, smiling softly: "Thus even Madana's pride did not survive(?)." *(Refrain)*
"Most wondrous is this golden complexion — an excellent intoxicating nectar: having drunk only a little of it,
the three worlds became intoxicated, forgot everything and made the son of Shachi the essence of their life.
The deliverer from the burning heat and darkness of people's minds, the abode of mercy, the moon [among] His fine associates —
[He has come, and] the word 'sorrow' on earth has passed beyond hearing; the world is filled with great bliss.
The opposite of joy has vanished; now Kali and sin with [all] their retinue are put to shame.
Hari, Hari! Who [now] is left without the right?" — says Narahari. — O Lord, [such is] Your might![^04-17]

*Raga yatharaga*

**4.14.** The Father of Achyuta, the refuge of people, renowned in the world as generous, the remover of the sorrows of the wretched,
cherishes in His mind so many, many wishes; [now] He loses His composure, [now] again He restrains Himself.
With powerful longing, like a pauper, without hesitation — the king of the twice-born in His night attire
He took by the path of the eyes and with great care seated on the seat of His heart.
Offering kadamba flowers — [His] feelings — He worships [Him] and offers body and mind to Him in nirmanchhana;
crying "Jaya, jaya!", unnoticed, He dances softly — stealing people's minds;
every moment saying "I have won, I have won!" He rejoices, by Himself immersed in sweet rasa.
Seeing this incomparable sight, — who can tell the limit of the happiness of Narahari, Haridasa and the others?[^04-18]

*Raga yatharaga*

**4.15.** Behold the excellent form of Lord Advaita: who fashioned [Him] — no one can understand.
Is it not a heap of champaka and shona(?) flowers? In each of [His] limbs, it seems, Ananga has taken shelter.
His lovely face shatters the pride of groves of blossoming lotuses; a soft, sweet smile;
lips deep red, bestowing delight; an incomparable golden effulgence, a radiance beyond compare.
His eyes are pure, wide, full of rasa; [their] expressiveness conquers the world; the brows are charming;
the nose is lovely, the forehead and ears graceful; the cheeks steal the pride of golden mirrors.
The neck is well formed, like a conch; the beautiful arms reach to the knees, lovely;
seeing the gleaming, full, broad chest, no one has the strength to keep composure.
A wondrous deep navel; the fine hairs — like a camphor creeper — shine endlessly.
Gracefully clad in Chinese silk, most resplendent is the waist that humbles [the pride of] the lion.
The thighs — [like] inverted plantain trunks — are supremely charming; the pair of fine ankles, giving joy, is incomparable.
The red soles [crush] the hosts of lotuses(?); to the radiance of the jewel-like nails Ghanashyama offers himself.[^04-19]

*Rangamala-chhandah*

**4.16.** Beside Gaurachandra wondrously shines the root of joy — the son of Madhava;
his charming radiance cannot be described even a little.
His beautiful, excellent grace intoxicates the world; his night attire is spotless;
rows of horripilation sparkle on [his] body, [tender as] a shirisha flower.
His lotus eyes are full of languor and slightly reddish; a lovely face;
the lips are clear — as if he were always softly smiling.
His charming garment shines; his thick locks are slightly loosened;
his composure lost, Narahari meditates [on this] every morning.[^04-20]

*Raga yatharaga*

**4.17.** Gadadhara — the foremost connoisseur, the abode of rasa.
Charming is [his] golden body; to the delightful radiance of [that] body how many Kamas offer themselves in nirmanchhana! *(Refrain)*
His lotus face conquers forests of lotuses; his lovely soft smile is like flower nectar;
again and again filling and filling the cups of their eyes, [all] drink [it] — great jubilation in the heart.
His soft, sweet, fresh words put to shame the excellent mantra that subdues the world;
hearing [them], the eager ears — [and even] the shrutis — long [for them] more and more; forgotten is the hearing of the Vedas and the teachings of the shruti(?).
Recalling his former deeds in his heart, he grows restless, loses composure and movement, and floats in great happiness.
Gold is left far behind; incomparable is [his] excellent prema; Narahari, seeing it — hidden [and yet] manifest — smiles.[^04-21]

*Raga yatharaga*

**4.18.** Sri Narahari is the king among the skilful.
He shines beside the son of Madhava; his bearing is a fitting match [for him], unseen in the world. *(Refrain)*
The moon of Gaura's face with its sweet smile — on it both [his] eyes are fixed with great delight;
his nose on the fragrance of [His] body, his fine ears on hearing the nectar of [His] words: [he] wishes for no interruption.
Seeing again and again the most charming, slightly loosened night attire, [he] feels great jubilation in his heart.
The way of prema is most wondrous, beyond comprehension: [to him] it seems the former pastimes in the forests of Vraja.
How many efforts he makes to keep his composure — [but] composure does not stay; [he is] ceaselessly restless.
His most tender body is overflowing with love; to [his] incomparable character Ghanashyama offers himself.[^04-22]

*Raga yatharaga*

**4.19.** Today's happiness cannot be described at all.
The rasika, steadfast, refined Srivasa, seeing the Lord's play, smiles very softly. *(Refrain)*
His stately body trembles, overflowing with love; graceful is [his] bearing, incomparable [his] great beauty.
Dense horripilation, [like] kadamba flowers, shines — as if he had put on a most charming jacket.
He trembles like a creeper in the wind, unable to steady himself — helpless, full of rasa.
Again and again, ceaselessly, he says: "Friend of the wretched!" — his heart melts, composure is far away.
He sings of [the Lord's] beautiful qualities, dances lightly, lightly; streams of tears flow from his eyes.
Narahari says: one cannot perceive how many, many transformations of bhava arise in his heart.[^04-23]

*Raga yatharaga*

**4.20.** Beautiful and refined is Gadadhara Dasa.
A jewel of virtues, he shines beside Gaura — like the moon's radiance beside the moon. *(Refrain)*
His most tender body is full of love; his sweet charm diminishes the pride of the champaka;
his bearing, which shatters composure, delights the world; his lotus feet stand motionless(?).
Both his eyes, full of languor, most charming, tremulous, — do not blink even for a moment;
his pure cheeks sparkle; how graceful the smile with his deep red lips!
Imperceptibly, ceaselessly, former feelings of many kinds arise in [his] heart;
however much he tries to conceal them — they cannot be concealed; Narahari, seeing [this], laughs, intoxicated with happiness.[^04-24]

*Raga yatharaga*

**4.21.** What shall I say? The associates are supremely magnanimous:
the nectar of the incomparable moon — Gaura's face — they drink ceaselessly. *(Refrain)*
So many, many efforts they make to keep their composure — [yet] every moment they grow restless, helpless, intoxicated with rasa.
Adorned with wondrous bhavas — abundant excellent ornaments — [they display] auspicious beauty of many kinds.
In someone the body bristles with horripilation, no word comes out — the beautiful(?) voice falters [with feeling];
in someone there is a trembling that makes people's minds tremble; from someone's eyes flow streams of tears;
someone whirls about, making gestures with his arms; someone utters the sweet name again and again;
someone laughs softly and dances again and again. — When will Narahari, seeing [this], spend [his life] fruitfully(?)?[^04-25]

*Shobha-chhandah*

**4.22.** Thus the refined associates
gaze again and again at the moon of Gaura's face.
Joyful delight arises; their minds are supremely glad;
[they] cherish various wishes, — says Narahari.

*Thus [ends] the fourth kirana of the Shrimad Gaura-charita-chintamani, called "The Description of [Gaura] Surrounded by Rows of Devotees, and Other Matters", in the description of the deeds of the morning period.*[^04-26]

[^04-1]: The name of the kirana is taken from its colophon at the end of the kirana: "…pratah-kaliya-charitra-varnane bhaktavali-veshtitadi-varnanam nama chaturthah kiranah" — "the fourth kirana, called 'The Description of [Gaura] Surrounded by Rows of Devotees, and Other Matters', in the description of the deeds of the morning period". The picture of Gaura among His associates as the golden mountain Sumeru surrounded by the gods is in 4.2. The subheading "Again, in the morning period…"

[^04-2]: The name of the metre is badly faded: it reads *ca…ṅgī chandaḥ*, probably *caturbhaṅgī*. The first line of the stanza is almost effaced: only *āju niśi bhora …* ("today, [when] the night has ended"), *gaura(?)*, *kautuka* ("joyful play") and single letters can be read; this line is translated by sense, with ellipses for the illegible words. The stanza is a dandaka (prose-like); the printed lines wrap arbitrarily, and in the translation it is divided at the marks "।" and "॥". Each segment ends with a rhyming epithet that refers to all those named in it: *ravi-karacchaṭā*, *jagata-viditā*, *prema-muditā*, *śrīkarā*, *gaurasevana-tatparā*, *dāsābhirāmā*, *śobhānupamā*.

[^04-3]: A list of the Lord's associates in Navadvipa; the division into names and the identifications are tentative (cf. note 68 of kirana 2). Shrinivasa is Srivasa Pandita (cf. note 15 of kirana 1); Gadadhara is named twice (Gadadhara Pandita and, probably, Gadadhara Dasa); Mukunda twice (Mukunda and Mukunda Datta); Narahari — Narahari Sarakara (or the author's signature); Pundarika Vidyanidhi; Shriman Pandita; Hiranya (Pandita); Gauridasa Pandita; *vāsudeva dattācārya* — probably Vasudeva Datta; Bhagavata — probably Bhagavata Acharya; Chandrashekhara Acharya; Shuklambara Brahmachari; Shridhara (the seller of banana-leaf cups); *śrīkṛṣṇadāsa paṇḍita bhagavān* — possibly "Krishnadasa Pandita, Bhagavan"; Buddhimanta Khan; Gangadasa Pandita (Nimai's teacher); Nandana Acharya; Shivananda (Sena); *ratna* (between Nandanacharya and Mukunda Datta) — unclear, possibly Ratnagarbha Acharya or an epithet; Satyaraja Khan and Ramananda (Vasu) of Kulinagrama; *vāṇīnātha miśra madhusūdana* — possibly "Vaninatha, Mishra Madhusudana"; Jagannatha Mishra — probably the Lord's father (the scene takes place in His house); *gopīkānta paṇḍita maheśa* — possibly "Gopikanta Pandita, Mahesha"; Govinda, Madhava and Vasudeva (Ghosha); Ishana — Shachi's servant; Kashishvara (Pandita). The names Govinda, Jagannatha and Krishnadasa occur twice in the list — so in the edition. Signature: Ghanashyama.

[^04-4]: "King of the twice-born" (*dvijarāja*) also means "the moon": Gaurachandra is the moon among the brahmanas. Padapitha — a low seat (cf. 3.11, 3.13). Shesha — the thousand-mouthed serpent who sings the Lord's glory (cf. 4.10: "neither Shesha nor Sharada").

[^04-5]: "A painted doll" (*citraka putari*) — motionless with wonder. Signature: Narahari.

[^04-6]: Raga lalita is a morning raga. "Dances" — *nirata kirata* (*nirata* = *nṛtya*; *kirata* probably for *karata*). The second line has no closing mark in the edition. "Crying aloud" — *phutakāri* (meaning unclear; possibly "sobbing"); grass between the teeth is a sign of humility. "Surging" — *su-uthalala*. Signature: Narahari.

[^04-7]: The moons are Gaura and His associates. The threefold misery (*tāpatraya*) comes from one's own body and mind, from other beings and from the forces of nature. Arena (*raṅga-bhūmi*) — the place of the wrestlers' contest; the asuras are those who have turned away from the Lord. "A field without a fence" — *bā̐dha kari rahita* (lit. "devoid of dam/enclosure"): the fruits of the wish-fulfilling trees are open to all. Signature: Narahari.

[^04-8]: Trivikrama is the name of the metre; the stanza is a dandaka. Reading and meaning unclear: *prakāśāja(?)-suṣamā* is taken as "the beauty of [His] manifestation"; *bara-barada-ānanda(?)* as "pouring down bliss" (possibly *baraṣad*); *dara na nirata* as "unceasingly". The thought of the first line: the courtyard holds countless people and yet remains spacious — by the inconceivable power of the Lord. (*bhaṇḍa*), "a bull without a stick" — the author's self-deprecating signature (Narahari).

[^04-9]: Sudhamukhi is the name of the metre. *alinda* — the terrace (veranda) in front of a house.

[^04-10]: Chitra is the name of the metre; the stanza is a dandaka. "Gaura-Govinda" — Gaura, Krishna Himself. "A cloud of joy" — *haraṣa meha* (*meha* < *megha*). "Waves His arms" — *kuruthe* (probably for Skt. *kurute*). "The purest gold" — *daśabāṇa susvarṇa* (*daśabāṇa* — gold of the highest assay). Anuraga — the ever-increasing attachment of love. "The insignificant fool Ghanashyama" — the author's self-deprecating signature.

[^04-11]: Belavali (in print *belābalī*) is a raga. Nityananda is Balarama, Krishna's elder brother; Gaura is His younger Brother. "A wondrous dark body" — the form of Krishna in Vraja; "a hidden rasa" — Gaura is Krishna in the mood and lustre of Radha. Nityananda's speech about himself: "my" in lower case (by the project's rule). Reading and meaning unclear: *prasanna baraṇe abanāgi* is taken as "propitiating [the Creator], I shall ask a boon". "A body made of eyes" — cf. the lament over the lack of eyes in 3.4. "Narahari's Lord" (*narahari pahũ*) — Nityananda as the author's Lord; signature.

[^04-12]: Veloara (in print *beloyāra*) is a raga (cf. kirana 1). The son of Padmavati is Nityananda (Padmavati is His mother). "The golden-hued thief" — Gaura, who steals Nityananda's ornaments and clothing, that is, His composure. The chakora is a bird that drinks moonlight. Signature: Ghanashyama.

[^04-13]: "May I be offered up to it" — *balihārī*, an exclamation of admiration. Shesha and Sharada (Sarasvati) are paragons of eloquence. The last lines express a well-known thought: Gaura's mercy is attained by Nityananda's mercy. Signature: Narahari Dasa.

[^04-14]: Here Nityananda's beauty is described through its effect on women — the same device of poetic hyperbole of beauty as in the descriptions of Gaura (cf. kirana 3, 3.3, 3.12). Gaudiya Vaishnavas, following Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati and the *Chaitanya-bhagavata* (Adi 15: in His lila the Lord did not jest with women or even look at them), reject *gaura-nagari-vada*, the worship of Gaura as an enjoying lover: Gaura is Krishna in the mood of Radha, tasting Her love, not an enjoyer. "Deceives the world" (*jaga-vañcana*, refrain) — meaning unclear: possibly "concealing His [divine] nature from the world" or "surpassing [everything] in the world". Shirisha is a tree with very delicate flowers.

[^04-15]: Ananga (Kama) is the god of love; his bow is a usual comparison for the brows. Garuda's beak — a comparison for a straight, handsome nose. Triveni — the confluence of three rivers (Ganga, Yamuna and Sarasvati) at Prayaga; here, the three folds of the abdomen. The blue garment is the garment of Balarama. *cīna* — fine (Chinese) silk. "Knees" — *uru parva* (cf. 3.3). The stanza begins and ends with "॥১১॥"; in print *barikhe* ("pouring") looks like *baribe*. Signature: Narahari.

[^04-16]: Veloara is a raga (as in 4.9–4.11; "again" — *punaḥ*). "Slaps both His armpits" — *kakṣa-yuga-vādya*, lit. "playing on both armpits": a gesture of jubilation with which Advaita, according to the *Chaitanya-bhagavata*, expressed His joy. "Corpulent" — *tundila*. Reading and sense unclear: *diśā(!) jayakṛta* (after *diśā* there is a mark resembling "!") — taken as "victorious in all directions"; *lahu bahu hasata* — perhaps for *lahu lahu* ("very softly"); *sughara lobha rijha āra* — taken as "the connoisseurs' longing and delight grow still more". Kunda — white jasmine: a comparison for the radiance of the teeth. "The Lord is overcome" — *pahuka parābhava jāni*: taken as "Advaita is overcome by prema" (possibly: "[the pearls] are defeated by the Lord['s tears]"). Signature: Narahari.

[^04-17]: The Lord of Sita (*sītāpati*) — Advaita; Sita Thakurani is His consort. The first line is centred, the second ends with the mark *dhru* (refrain). Sense unclear: *aiche madana-mada na rahala pora* — taken as "thus even the pride of Madana (Kama) did not survive" (before Gaura's golden beauty). Lines 3–8 are understood as Advaita's reflection. "Deliverer" — the first letter of the word is damaged: *?āraṇa* (*tāraṇa* / *vāraṇa* / *hāraṇa*); in any case the sense is "delivering, dispelling". "Passed beyond hearing" — *śravaṇa-gata*, taken as "is no longer heard". "The opposite of joy" — sorrow. "Who [now] is left without the right?" — *ko adhikāra hīna karu*: taken as "no one is left without the right [to prema]". Signature: Narahari.

[^04-18]: The Father of Achyuta — Advaita (Achyutananda is His son). "The king of the twice-born" — Gaura (*dvija-rāja*, also "the moon"; cf. note 4). "Like a pauper" — *raṅka-sama*: like a poor man eagerly seizing a treasure he has found. Nirmanchhana — see note 3 of kirana 3. "I have won!" — probably Advaita's triumph, whose calls brought the Lord into the world. "Immersed" — in print *bhora* (also resembles *tora*). Haridasa — probably Haridasa Thakura, a close companion of Advaita. Narahari — the author's signature woven into the list.

[^04-19]: Shona (*śoṇa*, print unclear) — cf. 3.3 (perhaps *sona*, "gold"). Ananga (Kama) — the "bodiless" god of love: his beauty seems to have found shelter in Advaita's limbs. "Blossoming lotuses" — *bikasita-kañja-bipina* (cf. 3.12). "Camphor creeper" — *kapūra-ballī*: the line of fine hairs above the navel. *cīna basana* — fine (Chinese) silk (cf. 4.11). "[Crush] the hosts of lotuses" — reading and sense unclear: *kamala kuladala laye* (or *naye*); taken as *kamala-kula dalaye* ("crush the multitude of lotuses"). "Offers himself" — *nichani* (the nichhani rite). Signature: Ghanashyama.

[^04-20]: Rangamala is the name of the metre; each line is printed in two parts. The son of Madhava (*mādhavātmaja*) — Gadadhara Pandita (Madhava Mishra is his father; cf. note 15 of kirana 1). Shirisha — a tree with very tender flowers (cf. 4.11). "Charming garment" — *sarasāṃśuka* (perhaps "moist garment"). Signature: Narahari.

[^04-21]: Gadadhara Pandita; "golden body" — *gaura-tanu*. Kama — the god of love: even many Kamas offer themselves in nirmanchhana to his radiance. Reading and sense unclear: *bhari bhari pari pīyata* (*pari* — "thus, like"?); *śunata lubadha śruti śruti bāñchata bahu bisarita beda śrabaṇa śrutitantra* — taken as "the ears, [and even] the shrutis (the Vedas), long [for his words]; the hearing of the Vedas is forgotten". "Former deeds" — the lila of Vraja: in Krishna's lila Gadadhara is Srimati Radhika (*Gaura-ganoddesha-dipika*). "Gold is left far behind" — *dūre rahu hema*: gold cannot compare [with his complexion]. Signature: Narahari.

[^04-22]: Sri Narahari — here Narahari Sarkara Thakura of Shrikhanda, the Lord's associate, inseparable from Gadadhara (in Krishna's lila — Madhumati, *Gaura-ganoddesha-dipika* 177), not the author's signature. The son of Madhava — Gadadhara Pandita (cf. 4.16). Sense unclear: *bhaṅgī susadṛśa adṛśa jagamājha* — taken as "his bearing is a fitting match [for him], unseen in the world". "The former pastimes in the forests of Vraja" — Narahari sees in Gaura and Gadadhara Krishna and Radha. Signature: Ghanashyama.

[^04-23]: Srivasa Pandita. "Jacket" — *kañcu*: horripilation covers the body like a garment. "Friend of the wretched" — *dīna-bandhu*, an address to the Lord. Signature: Narahari.

[^04-24]: Gadadhara Dasa — an associate of the Lord; in Krishna's lila, the radiance (*kānti*) of Radha (*Gaura-ganoddesha-dipika*), hence the comparison "the moon's radiance beside the moon". Reading unclear: *kañja-caraṇa gatihīna* (the first letter of *kañja* is not quite clear) — taken as "the lotus feet stand motionless". "Former feelings" — the moods of the lila of Vraja. Signature: Narahari.

[^04-25]: Reading unclear: *gadagada kaṇṭha su?āra* (the middle letter is unclear; perhaps *sutāra*) — taken as "the beautiful voice falters". "The sweet name" — the name of the Lord. Reading and sense unclear: *saphala goṅāba(?) kaba heri* (faint print) — taken as "when will [Narahari], seeing [this], spend [his life] fruitfully". Signature: Narahari.

[^04-26]: Shobha is the name of the metre (4.22). The colophon is read in the 1947 edition: «ইতি শ্রীমদ্গৌরচরিত্র-চিন্তামণৌ প্রাতঃকালীয়-চরিত্র-বর্ণনে ভক্তাবলী-বেষ্টিতাদি-বর্ণনং নাম চতুর্থঃ কিরণঃ ॥৪॥». Under strong magnification it clearly reads *bhaktāvalī-veṣṭitādi* ("[Gaura] surrounded by rows of devotees, and other matters") — without *r*. The reading *-ādi* agrees with the kirana's subtitle (*saundaryādi-varṇanam*, "the description of the beauty and other matters"). Kirana 4 comprises verses 4.1–4.22; kirana 5 begins.


## Kirana 5. The Deeds of the Morning: Description of the Love of the Elders and Other Matters[^05-1]

*Morning in Navadvipa: the elderly men and women of Nadia, who love Gaura as their own child, speak of Him to one another in secret, come leaning on their sticks to Shachi's courtyard, pray to the gods for His welfare, and, having drunk in the beauty of His face, become young again, as it were*

*Again, in the morning period — the excellence of the prema of the elders.*

*Raga vibhasha*

**5.1.** Ah, how wondrous! Who does not pine for the deeds of Gaurangachandra?
The people of Nadia, not seeing [Him] at night, cannot keep life [in their bodies].
Asleep, in their dreams they know nothing else — they regard Gora as their all;
